ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e155	The size treats today Report. St. 16 for 2015–2016, From Exclusion to New Chance – Coordinated efforts for adult learning. I would like to thank the Committee for a good cooperation in the work of the message. The aim of the message is to develop a coordinated and comprehensive policy for adults with low education, weak basic skills or unacknowledged competences. A single committee points out that there is a clear link between basic skills and participation in the labour market. Many of those with poor reading skills or low skills are just out of work. At the same time, we know that the working life of the future imposes higher standards of competence. Most people have to change, change jobs and constantly update their own knowledge and skills. This is why it is an important objective to have good systems to increase the overall competence of the adult population. Adults are in a different situation than children and young people in the ordinary course of education, and we need more flexible offers where work and training are combined. Asylum seekers and refugees can be a resource for society. The assumption is good language skills and basic knowledge of Norwegian society. In order to maximise the ability to take up work and become well integrated, it is necessary to strengthen and make the offer of immigrants and asylum seekers more effective. The Working Party is pleased that the Committee points to the need for rapid monitoring of the competences of immigrants, proper qualification and training tailored to individual needs. The message bears the impression that it was high time for a big message about lifelong learning. It cleans up in a number of circumstances that are urgently needed in the sector. In particular, there are opportunities for training for adults with poor basic skills and better access to secondary education for adults. Examples of issues on which there is a broad consensus are the mapping tools for basic skills, the strengthening of the system for the recognition of foreign vocational education and training, the possibility of real competence assessment in languages other than Norwegian, professional letter at work and the provision of better and more flexible access to higher education. The most positive part of the message is the coordination that has been initiated between different departments in this field, and that it is said that lifelong learning needs to be seen in the context. If we are to be honest, there is not much political conflict about this message. One important reason for this is that much in the message is little concrete and not very offensive either. Like several hearing bodies, the Workers' Party believes that many of the proposals contained in the message are little concrete. It makes it hard to have a bastant opinion about a lot of what's being written. The practicalisation of the proposals will therefore have a great deal to say about how both the sector and the parties in this room will deal with the measures. The government says on a number of points that they will decide on different aspects of several of the proposals when preparing the regulations. Therefore, the follow-up and concreteisation of the message by the government will be important. An example of this is that the Labour Party has some concerns about the direction in which modulation of secondary education will take. The purpose of modules and a more flexible offer is good, but it is worth listening to, among other things. LO's concern, that here we must be on guard that it will not be opened up for employers to ask their employees to take only individual modules or parts of a course, so that we receive A and B training. It must remain a requirement that the aim of secondary education be final competence, i.e. letter of formal qualifications, letter of association or study competence. This is a message in which the Labour Party believes that more could have been done, that a much broader strategy on lifelong learning and a much more thorough work in the field of integration should have been put forward. Now we know that the government has announced that there will be a strategy of competence. It is positive and has been well received. But why do you choose to run these processes so broken and divided instead of seeing them in context? In today’s and future working life, everyone will need to update their own knowledge and competences regularly. The learning of adults does not apply and should not be reserved for those who can be characterised as marginalised groups on the labour market or for adults with weak basic skills. We need a holistic approach in this area.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e181	It is a historic big message we are discussing today. This Communication is the first of its kind in which the government has coordinated a comprehensive policy for adults with low education, weak basic skills or unacknowledged competences. Many of these challenges go across sectoral lines, and the Communication has therefore been developed jointly between the Ministry of Knowledge, the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs and the Ministry of Justice and Civil Protection. The notification has three main areas of activity: better training for adults with poor basic skills, better access to secondary education and better quality of the specific offers to immigrants. The Communication will contribute, through these three main areas of action, to creating precisely better access to adult training and a better basis for cooperation between education, integration and labour market authorities. In this way, I would like to say that the complexity of this message and the fact that it is the first of its kind confirm that this is a work that also needs to go a little bit further in the sector as you develop offers and get them arranged as best as possible and as targeted as possible. There is a clear link between basic skills and participation in working life. Adults with low skills participate less than other adults in education and working life. Some 560,000 persons between 25 and 66 years of age in Norway have the highest completed education. This corresponds to about 20 pst. of the group. About 400,000 people, or about 12 pst. by Norwegian adults, have poor reading skills, according to the international study of literacy, i.e. PIAAC. Many of these are outside the labour force or have weak links with it. The working life of the future places higher demands on skills, and most will have to expect to change, change jobs and more frequently update their own knowledge and skills. A lifelong learning policy must therefore embrace a broad range, involve the whole of public services and include working life and the voluntary sector in the work. Therefore, the right now looks forward to making it easier for adults to receive training while in work and to making relevant training available in several arenas. Many adults need to combine education with work and other obligations, which means that training for adults needs to be more flexible and adapted to the lives of adults. Refugees will also receive better Norwegian training and greater opportunities to take education while learning the language. Many refugees and immigrants need Norwegian training and other qualifications to get work. To date, it has been difficult to combine Norwegian training with participation in primary or secondary education. Norway has a responsibility to enable asylum seekers and refugees who are allowed to stay to participate in the labour market, in order to ensure good integration and use the resources of immigrants. The right considers it essential to effectively monitor the competence of migrants, to provide qualification where possible, and not least to ensure proper training. We are therefore also looking forward to the government initiating preparatory adult education in the form of modular education at primary school level, and developing curricula for the trials that are better suited to adult needs, including for adult immigrants. Similar tests shall also be carried out for adults in the field of education and will be provided for selected vocational training programmes. It is likely that modular education will be a way of making it easier for some to take on training when appropriate, and in this way you can gain competence in a subject without having to take on training in the parts of the subjects you have already completed. In this way, we will ensure that more people are able to demonstrate their skills in the course of training, while ensuring that their final competence is of the same quality as in the ordinary course of training for professionals. This will contribute to increased flexibility, which will better safeguard the target audience of the offer and can be an important tool for more people to complete and pass on higher education. It is important that the offer is made in several arenas, so that it is available for the most part. Finally, voluntary organisations and study associations are also important in providing adult training and, with different devices, can be an important complement to ensure basic skills and inclusion, including by immigrants.

The Progress Group is pleased that there is now a communication on coordinated efforts for adult learning. This is something the Progress Party has been calling for for many years. This Communication is the first in its kind in which a government has developed a coordinated and comprehensive policy for adults with low education, weak basic skills or unacknowledged competences. We believe that all people have the potential to get better. The world is evolving, society is evolving, new demands and needs are created. This makes skills development and lifelong learning even more important today than it was before. The Progress Party will therefore underline the importance of investing in adult learning for all groups in society. The Progress Party is pleased that the importance of cross-sectoral cooperation is emphasised and that cooperation with industry is emphasised. We look forward to making it easier for adults to receive training while they are at work and that relevant training is made available in several arenas. The notification has three main fields of action which together will provide better access to adult training and a better basis for cooperation between education, integration and labour market authorities: better training for adults with poor basic skills better opportunities for higher education better quality in the specific offers to immigrants There is a need for more flexible and individualised training for adults that can accelerate transition to work and/or higher education. Among other things, pilots of pre-adult education are being launched through modular, primary and vocational education. Among adults between 25 and 66 years of age, about 560,000 people have primary education as the highest level of education. Further training, in particular vocational and vocational training, increases opportunities for stable footholds in the labour market. Training of a certain duration, which may provide formal competence, also has a good effect on the transition to work for job seekers and persons with disabilities. The Progress Party is therefore pleased that the government will extend the right to higher education. The level of competence and the need for training vary considerably among those with a migrant background. A high proportion of immigrants have low education and weak basic skills. In addition, they need Norwegian training. In this area, action must be taken to improve the ability of participants to acquire the skills requested in the labour market, including: by increasing the right to work. The Progress Party will stress the importance of allowing more people to receive training in combination with work, and is pleased that the government will establish a system of professional letters at work in cooperation with the social partners as an alternative route to professional and bachelor letters. The Progress Party will also point out that changes to the Training Act have been notified which make the letter of practice a formal opportunity in higher education and which obliges all counties to offer training for the purposes of letters of practice. The Party of Progress has noted that the Government of the notified notification of integration will assess the device of the introductory programme. This is today a system of highly varying quality, and the Progress Party is pleased to have a goal of improving it. Norway has lacked a central recognition for foreign vocational training and vocational education, which has made it more difficult to get asylum seekers and migrant workers into the labour market quickly. The Progress Party supports the establishment of national authorisation schemes for these fields and considers it natural that NOKUT should be responsible for such a scheme. Finally, the Progress Party will underline the special responsibility of Norway for small and protective crafts. Adult students without a right to secondary education constitute a vitally important student group for schools that provide this. The Party of Advances refers to the Government working on a change in the structure of supply in the professional sector and is pleased that recruitment to the small and protective crafts sector is specifically addressed in this context. The Progress Party hopes that the government will consider equalising adults seeking admission to small and protective subjects with applicants for youth rights, and that it will look into how the Law of Higher Education can better take care of the post-secondary schools offering such subjects.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e249	Report. St. 16 for 2015–2016 is an important big message because it highlights a serious societal challenge, namely adults who end up outside because they have too little education, too weak basic skills or not recognised competence. There is also a good message in many ways, in the sense that the government is alerting a number of good and sensible measures that it is easy to agree with. But this is a big message that has been notified for a long time, and to which there have been very high expectations, and I must say that although much is good, I am also a little disappointed, because I do not see it as a message that takes into account adults' learning in a broad sense, as is also evident from the department's limitations in the communication. However, when we look at the proposals and measures contained in the communication, much is good. I think it's great that the government will start testing pre-adult education in the form of modular education at primary school level. I think this will make it easier for many to acquire the skills they need. I am pleased that the government will consider amending the Training Act so that adults who have completed higher education abroad but have not been recognised in Norway will be entitled to secondary education. I believe that this should also apply to those who have not achieved an approved final qualification in Norway. It is positive that the government will establish a system of professional letters at work in cooperation with the social partners. I think it might be a good alternative way to work or work letters for some people. Then there's one issue that many of us have been dealing with for a long time, that finally gets a solution, and that's the relationship between youth and adult education. There has been a problem that many have exercised their juvenile rights and have had to wait for several years before they have been able to continue schooling. In the meantime, things may have changed. Some may have had a family and are in a completely different situation than they were before, and both the opportunity and motivation to return to school may have been weakened. It is therefore great that the government is now proposing that the right to secondary education be directly transferred to adult law. So if I'm going to say a little bit about what I miss in this message, it's a little bit broader about education and competence. Our education system consists of much more than the formal educational institutions. Here I want to bring forward the voluntary sector. Voluntary organisations and study associations are very important contributors in the development of competences in Norway. Many organisations offering courses and studies have a completely different opportunity to reach people living in what we call exclusion than the more formal parts of the education system have. Therefore, volunteering is not only important in terms of competence but also in terms of integration and inclusion. And I think that becoming a part of a volunteer organisation and participating in a course through a study association can both contribute valuable competence and at the same time be a very good gateway to the Norwegian community. For exclusion is not just about being out of work, it is also about social participation. The notification mentions the study associations, but is basically only mentioned. The government shows no ambition regarding how they want to facilitate the participation of study associations and voluntary organisations more actively than they do today. But the volunteer organizations are clear, they're forward-looking and offensive, and we're making a big mistake if we ignore them in this work. Universities are not mentioned at all, but the higher education institutions can also have a lot to contribute in terms of extraterritorial and adult learning. Personal maturation, development of self-esteem and master's expectations, social learning and preparation for education are, according to the research project 'Students' benefits from public higher education', the most important dividend for students to choose a year at the national college. I hope that the Ministry of Knowledge will follow up the dialogue established between them, the Swedish National College Council and the IMFI on how the National College can contribute to the integration work of young people coming to Norway as refugees.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e355	In the main message, the Council of State adds to the so-called modulation of initial and advanced training. He called from the pulpit now the letter of the gold standard, and that it should still be. Modulation can be good, as the needs of adults for adapted training are different from those of young people. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e536	I would first of all like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in dealing with this matter, which deals with a variety of changes in the Training Act. The proposal includes proposals to introduce freer choice of schools across county borders, to introduce a practice letter scheme in all counties and legislative proposals to facilitate the provision of more primary education for young people entitled to secondary education. In addition, the government proposes to establish a system for the recognition of foreign vocational training at secondary level, clarification in the law of when the training obligation expires in the event of foreign residence, and removal of the law’s recommendation on maximum school size. If any of the amendments are put together, the Committee is divided on other issues, so I assume that the position of the various parties will be made clear during the debate. I will now prepare for the position of the Christian People's Party in this matter. With regard to the question of free choice, there is little doubt that some students may need to attend a school in a state other than their home country. So freer school choices are basically a good idea. But if we introduce free schooling as a general right, I'm afraid that we may lose the opportunity to provide training in another state to those students who, for various reasons, actually need it. There will then be competition, mainly based on grades, and not an assessment of the individual student’s needs. That's why I think it's a much better idea to give a right to freer school choices, which will apply in fewer and clearly defined cases, such as: if the student lives closer to the school in his or her neighborhood than to the school in his or her own country, if there are special social or educational reasons for doing so, or if a student wants training which is not offered in his or her own country. A targeted right for those who need it is a better idea than a general right for everyone. In the case of letters of practice, I believe that this is an important alternative to offer to those students who lack the prerequisites or motivation to enter into an ordinary professional training course, and I support the government's proposal to provide all states with an obligation to offer this option. At the same time, there must be a goal of completing as much regular training as possible and of involving as many students as possible in ordinary apprenticeships after two years. With regard to the proposal to give the opportunity to offer more primary education to young people with a right to secondary education, I would just like to say that it is a proposal I applaud. Students with short periods of residence in Norway and a short period of time in primary school will have very poor conditions for success in higher education and for them to have a fair chance of success, we need to give them an opportunity to improve their diploma from primary school. At Thor Heyerdahl High School in my own county, Vestfold, a trial project has been carried out that has been tried out through a system called the Combination Class. The results have been good, not least in the form of lower non-responses. I hope that more municipalities and county councils will take advantage of the opportunity they now have, and that many will be inspired by what has been done in cooperation between Thor Heyerdahl High School, Larvik Municipality and Vestfold County Municipality. Also with regard to the proposal to establish a recognition system for foreign vocational training, I have to say that it is a proposal I think is good. The Christian People's Party also supports the proposal to clarify the rules on when the school obligation ceases to exist abroad. This is a limit that is basically important to clarify. But with regard to the proposal to remove the recommended maximum school age limit, I'm probably not as enthusiastic. I believe, like the Federation of Education, that the government underestimates the importance of this recommendation in the law. There may be a good economy in setting up larger and fewer schools, but it should be educational and not only economic evaluations that underpin the school structure. The Education Association points out that there are limits to the size of a school and that at the same time there are educationally sound practices. If the school becomes too large, there is a risk that the principal will only become an administrator and lose the ability to carry out pedagogical management and development work. This does not mean that it should not be possible to set up large schools, but in a sector that is experiencing a tight economy and ever-increasing demands for efficiency, I think it makes sense to give those who want to focus on the pedagogical assessments a little drag help. I therefore want to keep the legal wording as it stands. The Christian People's Party will vote against the proposed amendment of the law.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e553	Our goal is for all students to succeed and that the training offer in all counties is the best possible. The government will now impose an obligation on county municipalities to finance school places for their own students in other counties and to receive guest students from other counties in the event of free capacity. The Labour Party and the rest of the majority of the Committee, the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and the SV, want a different solution than government. Firstly, because the government's proposal for free schooling can favor students with good grades, not those who need the most to choose freers. Secondly, it is obvious that free choice of schools across county boundaries will make the job more difficult for the county councils that school owners. That is why most municipalities have expressed a negative opinion on this proposal. Dimensioning school provision and providing an optimal school structure is not an easy task at first. This proposal poses greater challenges for the counties to plan a proper number of student places, in particular the districts, and may stimulate the increase in county offers in direct competition with other counties. So even if there is so-called free capacity that controls how many guest students the states are taking in, some states will be able to increase their offer to attract students from other states. This may mean that some states will actually have to pay for other states to increase their capacity, and the consequence is that they may have to reduce supply or, in the worst case, put down lines and schools. A right for some students then leads to more students. That the government, the Right Party and the Advance Party, sees no problems with this, is alarming and is not a believer. This case also raises the question of whether the government believes that we should have a politically elected nationally governed school structure in Norway. Now there are two things happening at the same time — a private school law that opens up several private schools and introduces a far more freer school choice. Then it's harder to keep a popular management of the school offer. The overall responsibility of state policies is reduced. The working party is positive to give students a slightly stronger right to school places in other counties. However, the government's proposals have such inconspicuous and major implications for the county councils that the Workers' Party cannot support the government's line. A more sensible solution to enable those students who have a real need to go to school outside the home state to do so is the proposal agreed by the majority of the Committee. Students are given a stronger right to choose, but in fewer – and clearly defined – cases than the government provides, such as in cases where the home state does not provide the training required by the student, where students in border areas are given an unreasonably long internal travel route, which we have seen examples of, where another state has school places closer to the student's home, where there are specific social or educational reasons, or to ensure that Sami students or students with female and Finnish as their mother tongue receive a good offer. If free schooling had been opened up in these cases, the problematic cases we have seen in the media would have been avoided, but without compromising the role of county authorities as school owners. Finally, in another case that the Church, Education and Research Committee has dealt with this week – the absenteeism limit – we have heard representatives from Right-wing arguers strongly that we should all listen to what the teachers and the Federation of Education say and arrive at. I have to say that if the Right Party and the Advance Party had done it consistently, we would probably have had a quite different school policy, among other things. with regard to the view on multiple teachers or competence requirements. In this case, the Education Association has clearly said no to abolish the recommended maximum size of the Education Act for primary schools. The Education Association believes that there are limits to the size of a school and, at the same time, that it is well-managed, and they point out that the principals of schools that are too large may lose their room for pedagogical management and development work. The fact that the teachers are opposed to this proposal does not seem to have moved the government parties to a significant extent. Their desire to listen to the teachers was strong earlier this week, now it seems that the Right Party and the Advance Party are back on their usual track.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e566	I would also like to take this opportunity to thank the rapporteur and the committee for their good cooperation in this matter. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e590	First of all, I would like to thank the rapporteur for his good cooperation. This proposition promotes many good proposals for various changes to the Training Act, including: on freer choice of schools across county boundaries and a system of letters of practice. The proposition intends to get more people to complete high school, as well as that we want more freedom of choice. The free choice of schools implies the introduction of an obligation for county councils to finance school offerings in other counties and an obligation to receive guest students under available capacity. Furthermore, it is proposed to provide the county councils with an obligation to provide a letter of practice system in addition to regular vocational secondary education. The proposal also contains legislative proposals to facilitate the provision of more primary school education by municipalities and counties to young people entitled to secondary education. Experience from a school in Larvik shows that this can lead to both increased quality and less dropout in high school. It is also proposed to establish a system for the recognition of foreign vocational training at secondary level, with NOKUT as operationally responsible. For us in the Progress Party, it is important that students who, for various reasons, want admission to secondary schools in counties other than the home state, have the opportunity to do so. The proposed amendment will give many students greater freedom of choice and competition for places is based on characteristics, an objective and good recording criterion. The Party of Progress will point out that this applies only if the host State has free capacity. We will also stress that individual students with special educational and/or social needs should be given priority and refer to the Ministry's justification in the text of the proposal: ‘The Ministry has pointed out in its hearing that there will be cases where individual lives have specific educational and social needs that should prevail over the principle of freedom of choice for all and character-based intake, and that such preferential rights should be regulated more closely in regulations.’ And on the other hand, ‘The Ministry sees that a purely character-based scheme could be easier to manage, but insists on the assessment that there may be cases where individuals have specific needs that should prevail over the principle of freedom of choice for all and character-based intake.’ This shows that students with special needs are well cared for in the case, although some claim the opposite. In addition to the fact that increased freedom of choice is always positive, there is a positive side effect of freedom of choice across county borders: it is some important and traditional subjects that are small and endangered by closure. These will be easier to maintain when secondary schools have a higher student base than is available in each country. The proposal therefore involves both increased freedom and diversity – it is good Progress Party policy. The Progress Party will recall that the announced regional reform will address even more the need for larger inputs, but underlines that that reform process is at a very early stage. There is therefore a need for changes in the law that deal with the current state of the art. The Progress Party considers that the practical letter is a very good alternative to the ordinary professional training course for individual students and indicates that the evaluation of the scheme by the NIFU concludes that it has contributed to increased implementation and lower non-responses than could otherwise be expected from the participants in the programme. Among other things, the practical letter system has very good results in Rogaland. The Progress Party also believes that the aim should be to ensure that as many students as possible carry out an ordinary professional training course, but that at the same time it is important to have alternatives. The Progress Party will warn against the devaluation of the practice letter scheme presented by SV and others. There is a great diversity among students, and everyone does not fit the standard course. Then it is important that the school offers a variety of schemes that provide good job opportunities afterwards. Finally, I would like to point out that through several different school reforms, responsibility for the school has been increasingly transferred to the municipalities. In a long-term country such as Norway, there are significant local and regional differences. Then it is natural that communities themselves should design their school solutions. To find good school solutions locally, school size will only be one of many factors. Student base, industrial structure, area, infrastructure and access to competence are other factors involved. This is a field in which local politicians have the best overview. The Progress Party is confident that local politicians will find the best solutions, and therefore considers that there is no need for a centrally imposed standard of school size.

Finally, we are opposed to removing the law's recommendation on maximum school size. We believe that it would have been wise to keep this maximum limit, so that no pedagogical assessments could fall under the shadow of economic considerations in municipalities with a tight economy.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e918	It has been a pleasure to be the case rapporteur on this matter, which deals with changes in the law of higher education on a number of different points. The government is proposing changes such as: are about the quality development of institutions, and how supervision is to be organised is about the right to facilitate the work of fellow students after a doctoral degree is about the creation of a digital diploma portal, as well as several other different provisions of the law; I would like to thank the Committee for good and constructive cooperation and point out that there is a great deal of consensus and support for the Government's amendments. Several of the legislative proposals proposed by the government promote in the proposal follow from the structural communication, which we dealt with in the Storting last year, where the government announced a review of the legislation to ensure that the institutions carry out adequately the quality work resulting from the technical powers. One of the changes made is to extend the board of directors of NOKUT, which is the national body for quality in education, with one member, in order to ensure representation also from the vocational school students. So from now on, students from both universities, colleges and colleges will be represented on the board of NOKUT, and that's both fair and fair. Another step is to change the model of NOCUT’s supervisory and accreditation work to clarify that NOCUT is to oversee the overall quality performance of institutions. An overall committee is in favour of increasing the emphasis on quality, but we note the input that has come to raise the problem that the quality concept is not clearly defined in the law. To this end, the Committee clarifies the need for good involvement of universities and colleges in the operationalisation of the concept of quality work by the NOCUT before the supervision of the new model is carried out. With regard to changes in the calculation of the duration of service for fellows, it is about correcting what I believe is a bias in today's system. After today's legislation, doctoral education at universities and colleges is considered to be a duty period under the Civil Service Act. It has the unfortunate consequence that grant holders often do not have short-term exposures right after completion of education and that institutions must expel promising candidates, unless they have a firm position to offer. There has also been an important point for the government to facilitate open competition for scientific positions in the sector. Strengthening the quality of higher education is a major priority for the government, and this legislative proposal is a small piece of the major work currently being done in the sector. The government has presented a long-term plan for higher education and research, with bold objectives and quality as a keynote. The structural changes in the university and higher education sectors are also aimed at improving the quality and quality of our higher education institutions. In the government’s work on new teacher training, one of the steps was to conduct national delectionary examinations in some subjects or subjects. The aim is to be able to compare studies between institutions in order to use each other’s experience to further develop the study offer. It could make valuable contributions to improving the quality of the higher education sector. For this reason, the government proposes to introduce a general authorisation to be able to impose on institutions national delectionary examinations on some subjects. It is worth pointing out the good experience of NOCUT from national bachelor’s degree programmes in auditing and accounting and bachelor’s degree in nursing, but it is not a goal to introduce national delectionary examinations in any context. Opinions have been shared on the use of national delectuaries, and it is important to stress that this tool must be used wisely and in close cooperation with the professional communities. Finally, I would like to mention the establishment of a national diploma and character portal, which will contribute to a safer and simpler way for candidates to make their results visible to potential employers. Overall, these are legislative changes that, in total, contribute to improving the quality of higher education. The very best thing we can do to ensure value creation and welfare in the future is to invest in education and research, where good quality must be at the heart of it. That's what the government does in a broad sense. The government strengthens schools and teachers, and we strengthen both investment and quality in higher education and research. As I said, this amendment is a small piece in one of the main government projects, which is about laying the foundation for what we're going to live on in the future. And that's knowledge.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e958	The case rapporteur has given us a good overview of what this amendment to the law is about, so I don't want to spend time on it. On the other hand, I would like to thank the rapporteur for his good cooperation in the Committee, which has needed clarifications from the Committee. Most of the laws proposed by the government are of an unproblematic nature, as the Labour Party sees it, with one exception. The changes related to national delexams are the point where there has been the most exposure in advance. The reason for this is that for a few subjects and studies in Norway, education is expected to be more or less the same in its entirety, or on selected subjects is almost the same. Some subjects are managed by detailed framework plans, and some professional subjects are subject to authorisation. In other words, there is a expectation of a specific competence and a level of knowledge for the person who has passed the examination. At the same time, this is a subject which, like all other subjects, must have academic freedom. This can be seen to conflict with each other. This is particularly true of teacher training that the debate has taken place. New framework plans for primary school teacher training will now be put in place and, in this context, the government wants to make greater use of the national delec­tionary syllabus. That is the background to the bill. The working party sees advantages in introducing such an examination in carefully selected subjects. It may help to ensure increased quality and it will be possible to compare more closely the same study at different institutions. However, in the hearing replies, there was, in part, strong opposition to the introduction of such national delec­tionary examinations. This justifies several instances that academic freedom is threatened by such a standardised examination which must be equal irrespective of the place of study and institution. In addition, pedagogical disciplines warn against the risk that education will be adapted to the examination form and that the examination form will be more focused than the professional assessment of the learning outcomes. There is an objection I believe is highly relevant. Therefore, national delectuaries must be used with care and only where it serves the purpose. The working party is pleased that the Stortinget emphasises that national delectionary examinations should only be used to a limited extent and primarily in subjects governed by framework plans etc. Pilot projects have already been launched, and the experience gained from pilot projects must be emphasised in the possible continuation of these and the introduction of the sub-assemblies in new training. In order to succeed in making changes with great resistance, it is essential to cooperate closely with those amendments to which they apply. If national delectionary examinations are to succeed as intended, it is important that the professional communities have an influence on their development and implementation. Good anchoring in professional environments will prevent the introduction of the national delec­tionary panel from interfering with institutions’ study provision in an premature manner or leading to a uniformisation of education. Then I hope that we will get national delectuaries in the subject where they are suitable, and that we refrain from doing so in the subject that is not well-founded. It is therefore important that pilot projects are properly reviewed and evaluated and that the Stortinget be briefed on this. I am pleased that there is no support for the government's original proposal for almost a blank mandate to impose such national delectuaries on all subjects. Such national delectionary examinations may work in specific disciplines, in some fields of education, but opening them up in all disciplines seems unnecessary to me, and it seems rapidly provocative in a sector that feels that academic freedom is being pushed from different walks of life. I am therefore pleased that the setting clearly limits the possibility of introducing national delectionary examinations in general. Finally, a few words about temporaryity and changes made to the calculation of the duration of service of fellows. The proportion of temporaryity is too high in the UH sector. It is worrying, and there is even more cause for concern that the proportion has increased in the last two years. In this way, the sector does not appear to be attractive to graduates, and it is very unfortunate since we need the most competent academics into the research environments. I would like to point out that there is disagreement between the Ministry and the social partners on how this change in the calculation of service time will turn out. The fear of researchers may lead to an increase in the use of temporaryity. The ministry is of a different opinion. I would ask the Council of State to take this issue very seriously, as well as to monitor developments. If it turns out that the Swedish Research Association and the NTL are right in their concerns, the Council of State should consider countermeasures quickly and, where appropriate, be willing to reverse the re-organisation of the calculation of the duration of the service of fellows.

A specific treatment within a national complaint body will ensure national non-discrimination and ensure the legal certainty of the vocational school students. It is also an important integration measure. Overall, legislative changes will help to strengthen quality and attractive vocational education for students, and to ensure that students are treated in a non-discriminatory way regardless of the type of institution they choose to attend. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1380	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation during its work. In this case, some legislative changes are proposed. With regard to the amendments to the Children's Act, it is not a unanimous vote by the Committee, and I would like to explain the majority's views on these issues. Families have become more diverse. We have moms, dads, moms and cousins, foster parents, foster parents and adoptive parents to mention something. Children live well with this diversity as long as there is love and security around them. Children come with an amazing ability to make people around them love them, and we adults, most of us at least, are concerned about taking care of children around us regardless of biology. The families are more diverse, but nevertheless there is something firm, and it is our desire to know who is our biological mother and father. We have no time limits for moving matters today if you are wrongly convicted as a father. Nor are children bound by deadlines in these cases. The same is not true of a man who is a legal father and who can provide information suggesting that another man may be a biological father to the child, or a third party who believes that he is the father of a child who already has a legal father. In both cases, the cases must be raised within one year after they became aware of the information that the legal hazard could be incorrect. In addition, the third party must raise a case before the child reaches the age of three. The introduction of these deadlines was made on the grounds that stability in children's lives was more important than knowing the truth about the biological origin. These are not simple decisions, and ensuring the stability and security of a child's life is important. Nevertheless, the majority today will vote to remove these time limits, because the truth is more important. It will of course be stable to believe all your life that someone is your father, who is not, but the intention of the law should not be to build up under this form of stability, a stability that may be almost based on a lie. It is easy to find examples of fathers who are suddenly told that they are not a father, of mothers who carry tough secrets, or of children who realize that something is creeping, but who do not fully understand what it is, before they become adults themselves. If you read stories in women's magazines of the type "this happened to me", or for that matter, you can see that the stories of reality are reflected in culture as well. In fact, as in the fiction, these are difficult things. There may be many reasons why the lie has been told or stories are not corrected – everything from cheating to assault, from revenge to a desire to give the child the best dad. This makes these matters extremely difficult at human level, but the legal majority nevertheless believes that we cannot allow ourselves to make legislation that can prevent the truth from coming for a day.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1431	Today, when the Storting examines proposals to suspend deadlines for raising a case on changes in hazard, it is based on a recognition that knowledge of biological association is one of the pillars of life. Today we have technical possibilities to establish a relationship with nearly 100 pst. safety. If there were any doubts as to whether a father really is the biological danger, or whether a child might have another father, then the uncertainty would repel the established relationship between those who doubt. Today, a man who is a legal father can sue for fathership if he can provide information indicating that another man may be a biological father to the child. A man who believes he is the biological father of a child who already has a legal father may raise a case if he can provide information that indicates that he may be the father of the child. In both cases, a case must be raised within one year after they became aware of the information that the legal hazard may be incorrect. In addition, the person who will demonstrate his biological hazard must raise a case before the child reaches the age of three. There are no deadlines for children who want to file a case for a change in hazard. There are also no deadlines for convicted fathers who want to have their case resumed. Until 2003, there were time limits in the Children’s Act to bring up a case on changes in fatherhood. The Bondevik Government took the initiative to suspend the deadlines. The justification for the current rules on time-limits, introduced by the Stoltenberg II administration, was the consideration of stability and tranquillity for the child, although an unknown specific example of the absence of time-limits that would have been problematic for children. Although the truth for many, both adults and children, will often be liberating and positive over time, it does not seem possible on a general basis to say what is best for the child in the individual case. It concerns either the outcome should be a confirmation or a confirmation of an existing legal hazard. I believe that the importance of biological association needs to be given to this, and that the deadlines in the law prevent this. The time-limits prevent men who have become fathers as a result of marriage or because they have taken on danger may bring proceedings for a change in the hazard at a later stage. The deadlines also prevent other men from demanding that they be the father of the child, after the child has reached the age of three. If the mother and the child's legal father oppose the matter, the question of who is a biological father will not be resolved. Who is the biological parent of a child is absolutely fundamental to human understanding of himself. I believe that if there is any doubt as to who is the father of a child, it would be best for the child to have this question clarified. The importance of knowledge of biological origin is so heavy here that I believe there should not be deadlines in the law that prevent these matters from being properly addressed and dealt with in the judicial system. That is why I am pleased that the majority of the Committee supports the proposal to abolish the time limits for dealing with hazards.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1581	First of all, I would like to thank the representatives of Kirsti Bergstø, Heikki Eidsvoll Holmås and Karin Andersen for this representative proposal, which contains several good proposals. In the nomination, we point out that researchers from the Swedish Institute of Public Health and the University of Oslo have shown that twin mothers have 77 pst. greater risk of depression five years after birth than other mothers. The researchers suggest that twin mothers do not initially have worse mental health, but that depression is a consequence of stress, and that there is reason to assume that twin mothers become more tired than other mothers. Twin and multiple births in themselves involve a strain – with more children at the same time, and in addition, twins may have lower birth weights, premature births or complications such as brain bleeds and breathing difficulties. In the light of the findings, researchers conclude that longer leave for twin parents could be an appropriate measure. Multiple parents are entitled to five or seven weeks extra parental leave for each additional child they receive, depending on whether they choose 100 pst. or 80 pst. coverage rate. Parents for vibrations are entitled to 10 or 14 weeks extra, while parents for quadruples will be entitled to 15 or 21 weeks extra leave. Parents may choose whether to take out these extracts so that they are at home at the same time or to extend the period of benefit. Also, in ten weeks' time, Dad can take home while Mom's at home. The government wants municipalities to have power and responsibility so that they can provide good services to their inhabitants. We don't want to regulate the municipal business in detail. Other schemes are universal to all parents, such as the right to parental leave and reduced working time. It can also be an important part of this work.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1786	The background to this legislative proposal is Decision of the EEA Joint Committee No. 97/2016 to amend Annex XVI to the EEA Agreement on public procurement. The draft law deals with the three so-called acquisition directives and they are considered to be EEA-relevant. The majority of the Committee, all with the exception of SV, aligns with the assessments made in the proposal, and the majority recommends that the Storting should consent to the approval of Decision No 4 of the EEA Joint Committee. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. The matter has also been referred to the Foreign Affairs Committee, which has joined the referral. Otherwise, I refer to the position of the Business Committee in the next public acquisition case.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1797	These directives we are discussing today, entered into force in the EU in 2012. At that time, it might have been appropriate to find out what experience public authorities in the EU have had with the directives. The EEA offer of equal treatment for companies in 30 countries is expensive and heavy for many municipalities and public authorities. It takes working time and other resources to get involved in the bidding process, to design the tender documents so that they can be published internationally, and to carry out assessments of the offers that come in, in such a way that no complaints can be made. This supply regime is as painful in the EU as it is in Norway, the EEA. This is confirmed by a major study from EIPA, the European Institute of Public Administration, from 2009. The study has taken four EU countries plus Norway and Iceland and criticises that procurement rules limit local freedom to choose the way in which service provision is organised. According to CEMR, the Council of Europe Forum for Municipalities and Regions – the Norwegian KS umbrella organisation – the purchasing rules have become so complex that they are more harmful than useful. In a hearing in the European Parliament, CEMR spokesperson concluded that the bidding system has become more expensive, more complicated and more conflict-making for all parties. For example, the regulatory framework triggers: ever-increasing legal proceedings were brought by those who lose their bids. As a result, clients have to work hard to ensure that they are not withdrawn from the courts. Legal security often becomes more important than the design of the offer so that the assignment can be carried out in the best possible way. The legal proceedings lead to delays, insecurity and increased costs for both the contractor, the supplier and the developer. The demand for supply would make it cheaper for the public, but in practice has often led to the opposite. A study in England conducted by the KS’s sister organisation showed that two-thirds of the municipalities and regions surveyed believed that costs and administrative burdens had increased. In 2009, CEMR adopted a Charter of Local and Regional Services which advocates a strong delineation of the tasks that should trigger supply at EU level. According to the authorities, a basic right must be given to determine when to outsource a contract to an open tender, and when not to. Services of a local nature shall not trigger such an offer, according to CEMR. In addition, the EU must clarify the distinction between commercial and non-commercial services. The latter are in principle not covered by the supply requirement, but the EU has made it extremely unclear what is considered non-commercial. But the most important requirement for CEMR is that it must be up to the municipality what is going to be put on offer, and what the municipality can do with its own add whatever the form of organisation. So there was a little bit about the experience in Europe with these directives we are going to adopt today, which have been discussed to a small extent – perhaps not at all – in the context of the treatment here on the Storting. Here in Norway, KS has found that with the new Enforcement Directive, lawyers are becoming more important than economists in the public procurement arena. This umbrella organisation for Norwegian municipalities and county councils is also deeply concerned about the «overimplementation» of Norway has opted for the purchase directives. We are going on an important point much further than the directives require of us. With today's legislation, a public contractor can distinguish between ideal and commercial performers when purchasing health and social services. This is going to be a highly unclear issue within the new legislation, which we are going to deal with next, and thus everyone who is voting for the implementation of these directives today will put the non-profit sector in a potentially difficult situation. It is against this background, the experience that has been gained in Europe, and the problematic situations that this has seen, among other things. non-profit sector, SV is in favour of rejecting these acquisition directives by applying the right of reservation in the EEA Agreement.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1829	I just want to say that the next case is the follow-up to these directives, and it is well known that SV is against the EEA and the EU and everything. But the entire committee has prepared for us to take the debate on what is the government's follow-up, and that's the next thing. I just wanted to make it clear. That is why no one else takes the floor in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1848	At the request of the Business Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 10 minutes to each party group and 10 minutes to members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e1855	As rapporteur, I would first like to take the opportunity to thank the Committee for its good and constructive cooperation. We've had a thorough treatment. Most of the proposals and comments are widely agreed on, and at my discretion we have improved the government's bill on a number of points. This matter has attracted considerable attention, as we see from the amount of footage, meetings and interest around the hearing. It is not so strange when we consider that the government purchased goods and services for about 460 billion. kr in 2014. This is about 14 pst. GDP. Due to the large volume of procurement to the public sector, this is an important customer for Norwegian business. Society is facing demanding change over the next decades, and the public sector needs to use its resources more effectively than today and at the same time offer safe and good services. Public procurement is an important incentive for the market and the Committee is committed to making public use of the role of purchasing to promote positive ring-effects. I think we have managed well to balance the importance of simplification in ensuring that important societal considerations can be met. In the open consultation of the nutrition committee, there were several who wanted more important considerations to be included in the purpose paragraph. Some called for consideration of competition, other social conditions. I think we have found a good solution when we now propose that the word ‘socially decent’ be included in the purpose paragraph, in order to stress that procurement should not only be effective but also address important societal needs. Such a need is environment. There is a cross-political consensus that the law should include a new environmental regulation aimed at strengthening climate and environmental considerations in public procurement. It is positive, and there is a clear notification from the majority of the Committee that Norwegian enterprises should not be subject to any competitive disadvantage due to spe­cific environmental requirements. Unfortunately, we see some examples of this today. The Committee also stresses the importance of life-cycle costs. The requirement for universal design also becomes clearer in the law. Another aspect that the Committee emphasises is innovation. Innovative acquisitions are important both for service production and for business, and it is evident from a recent Menon report. In addition, it can contribute to addressing major societal challenges, such as challenges in the care of the elderly or the transition to the low-carbon society. I am also pleased that a majority of the government asked how a risk regime for public acquisitions could be designed. Innovative acquisitions may give rise to higher risks and therefore a certain amount of risk relief is needed by the country. As previous rapporteur, I am well aware of this. It may not be possible to take the chance of innovative purchases if the risk is perceived as high. Then both the municipality and the business community can miss a lot. The issue that has probably received the most attention in the Committee's consideration is the relationship to the non-profit sector. I am pleased that, through the treatment, we have ensured that older and caring people will continue to receive good and safe health services from non-profit actors in the future, and an overall committee states that this will continue to be facilitated. It is also important that the Economic and Social Committee has, at my discretion, cleared up the uncertainty created by the government's legislative proposals on the ideal framework conditions. On the part of the Labour Party, we want to underline the important role of the non-profit sector, and we are committed to ensuring that they do not lose contracts with commercial players in the health and social sector. A Joint Committee decides that EEA legislation should exploit the space for action and that specific rules should be laid down on the acquisition of health and social services. It is important for the non-profit sector and the municipalities to ensure the necessary space for action as they wish. The Workers' Party, together with the Christian People's Party, the Centre Party and SV, wanted to go even further – by legislating that contracts could be reserved for non-profit actors. Unfortunately, we did not get the majority on that, but an overall committee has nevertheless given important leads through comments, and the vote is very clear: It is established that it will be socially appropriate to provide for the continuation of a reservation facility and that this will be implemented in the regulation within the EEA area of action. A majority of the Committee also considers that different cooperation models used in the EU should also be available for use in Norway, for example. as Denmark has done to what is called ‘extended self-regime’. This is what the government is asking to look at now. It has also been important for the Committee to ensure regulatory simplification. New procurement procedures that give rise to negotiations between purchasers and providers are important in order to improve procurement. It is also agreed that the government should consider raising the national thresholds, so that fewer contracts are subject to the procedural rules. The Committee also considers that Norway, as the EU, must facilitate the fragmentation of contracts in order to enable small and medium-sized enterprises to compete with large-scale projects. At the hearing, the government sent proposals for regulations limiting the number of stages in the supply chain to three. The Committee also considers that it is necessary to make it possible to impose stricter requirements on the number of paragraphs, which would be applicable in sectors exposed to work crime. In particular, the Kongsberg Group and the Joint Undertaking have been concerned that the acquisitions of the defence continue to be exempted from the law, and the Committee agrees. The Committee also considers that no more detailed provisions should be introduced for the supply sectors than those resulting from the EU Directive and that no obligation to publish acquisitions below the threshold for our actors in the water, transport and energy sectors should be imposed. This is important for simplification and to ensure a level playing field with other EEA countries. It is gratifying that a unanimous committee is also committed to strengthening gender equality in business and sees that public procurement can contribute to it. In my view, the Committee has also provided good leadership to strengthen the apprenticeship clause in the law. Several have indicated that the previous public procurement notification, St.meld. No 36 for 2008-2009, Good procurement, presented by Stoltenberg II in 2009, has been a good action plan for public acquisitions. However, developments are progressing very quickly. Society is changing, and the law is changing. Therefore, there is agreement that we need a new, up-to-date message. Of course, this does not mean that the implementation of what we are adopting today should be postponed. Finally, I would like to say a little about enforcement. There is broad agreement that the power of the KOPA to impose an infringement fee should be reintroduced, which will result in more effective enforcement. However, the Committee is concerned that any person who does what is necessary to avoid or avert losses, disadvantages, damage or errors shall not be automatically charged an infringement fee. I am promoting this proposal No. 7 today, the proposal for a new legislative text, on behalf of the entire committee. Finally, I would like to mention proposal No. 2, on which the Workers' Party is involved. Finally, I would like to ask that proposal No. 3 is converted into a transmission proposal.

That is why I am pleased that the Left has been able to make a strong contribution to bringing innovation into the law. This means that public contracts now need to be implemented in a way that promotes this. The size of the public market makes it necessary to exploit the innovation power that lies here, and I believe it is essential that public acquisitions contribute more to innovation and innovative solutions. However, innovative acquisitions lead to higher risks because the parties cannot have full control of the process or result, as is more so when contracting entities purchase shelf goods or order the same as the last. Risk is a result of probability and consistency, and although the grantor and the supplier can jointly minimise the likelihood of something going wrong, there will often be a residual risk. For this reason, I am pleased that there is a majority in favour of the government issuing a risk regime for public acquisitions, which can lower the threshold for rethinking in the public sector. It's very important. Under the applicable legislation, the purchaser must decide on the procedure to be followed before the contest is announced. In this way, operators know whether to conduct negotiations or not when to write the offer. However, in the proposed new procedure, purchases do not have to decide on this until after the offers have been delivered. Suppliers shall therefore make offers which they are obliged to make, without knowing whether the buyer makes the choice of supplier the basis of which, as the offer has been made, or whether the buyer is to negotiate with one or more operators. I am concerned that this could lead to many actors not offering, that unprofessional players will have a huge competitive advantage, and that the room for innovation in public acquisitions will be significantly less than intended. I would therefore like to remind you that a unanimous committee believes that the contracting entity should indicate in the notice whether it intends to negotiate or not. The left expects this to be followed up in regulations, so that we avoid unnecessary unforeseeability for the operators, an uncertainty which in the worst case is so great that a person is at risk of losing confidence in the competition for the mission. Today’s legislation allows contracts to be reserved for non-profit actors in the whole of the health and social field. As a result of a new EEA legislation requiring equal treatment of different groups of suppliers, it has been doubted whether this exemption rule can be maintained. In the new law, therefore, this reservation option is passed on only to a limited number of contract types, which in practice means that in the areas where the ideals are protected today, they will still be protected. I'm glad we've found a good solution for the ideals. Ideals are of great importance as providers of health and social services in Norway. They have special advantages and characteristics that are valuable to society, while having conditions other than large commercial and public actors. These are the values to be taken into account, while the Left considers it important to review the legislation once the pension issue in the non-profit sector has been resolved.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e2093	I want to put myself in line with representatives of the committee who thank the case-speaker Odd Omland for an excellent job. Norwegian industrial enterprises meet in several contexts what is called "sondernorse environmental requirements". These are demands that we are proud to have here in Norway. At the same time, however, it imposes additional costs on industry and weakens competitiveness as a result. Then it's a paradox that we in Norway have no corresponding environmental requirements when public purchasing industrial products. This means that reinforcement irons are bought from China instead of Mo in Rana, because it's cheaper. But as we know, China's industry is much more dirty. As a result, Norwegian public bridges, roads and cultural houses are partly built with material imported from low-cost countries with completely different environmental standards for industrial production. With today’s procurement legislation, those parts of the global industry that are not subject to stringent environmental requirements, which pollute the environment and beggar very negatively in the climate struggle, are thus granted a competitive advantage by Norwegian authorities. And not enough: We are thus actively undermining the industry we ourselves want to build. Get it, whoever wants it. SV supports all these systems of the whole heart. It is very important that the public provide risk relief in situations where Norway needs to change its path. The same arrangements do not have public actors. And that should be a little cross of thought. This leads to public actors refusing to use new technologies, new solutions and ideas, because it poses too much risk.
