ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e2137	The representative of Torgeir Knag Fylkesnes has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e2140	Today, the majority will consider the government's proposal for a new law on public acquisitions. The law and its regulations regulate the procedure to be followed by the public when purchasing goods, services and works. In 2014, the total public procurement of goods, services and works amounted to about 460 billion. kr. Large parts of these purchases are governed by the Acquisition Act. The main objective of the law is to ensure the efficient use of society’s resources in public procurement. When we are now discussing the design of procurement legislation, and not least the other social concerns to be addressed, I believe that it is an important perspective to have. The basic principles set out in the legislative proposal shall contribute to the achievement of the objective of good and efficient use of resources. Contracting entities shall act in accordance with the principles of competition, non-discrimination, predictability, reprobability and proportionality. The procedural law contained in the regulations is based on these principles. In recent years, there has been growing criticism that the procurement law is too detailed, complicated and formalistic, and that contracting entities have become more concerned with avoiding errors than with making good purchases. In addition, studies show that Norway is among the countries in Europe with the highest costs associated with their acquisitions. The main objective of the government has therefore been to simplify the legislation. There has been a need for a critical review to assess the rules that need to be maintained and which we can be ahead of or simplified. The EU has also adopted three new procurement directives to help increase regulatory flexibility, reduce administrative burdens and clarify legal issues. At the same time, the Committee on Simplification has proposed a number of important simplifications in the specific national part of the legislation, and I support a large number of their proposals. The public is a big player with a lot of market power, and although it is a goal to get the most value for money, there are also other values that public authorities should take care of. Suppliers who squeeze prices through crime, irresponsible employment or unsustainable solutions should not win competition for public contracts at the expense of serious suppliers with forward-looking solutions. For this reason, the Government has proposed to introduce new rules requiring contracting authorities to ensure that different societal considerations are properly addressed in the procurement processes. Contracting entities shall set up their procurement practices in such a way as to contribute to the reduction of harmful environmental impacts and promote climate-friendly solutions, where appropriate. In practice, this means inviting Norwegian companies to come up with smart, green solutions in response to public tenders. Contracting entities shall also have appropriate procedures in place to promote respect for fundamental human rights where there is a risk of breach of such rights. Crime in the labour market is an increasing challenge, and enterprises that systematically contravene laws and regulations are destroying business. Therefore, the Government will require contracting entities to pay and working conditions and not least to limit the number of stages in the supply chain. The work of getting more people to complete and pass higher education is important, and we see that too many students have no apprenticeship. At the same time, we see an increasing need for the skills of professionals. We need more skilled workers, and then it's also our job to do what we can to make sure that they get an apprenticeship, get into a business and get the necessary skills. For the government, it has therefore been an important point to strengthen the requirement for apprentices in the new law. Now it no longer holds that the company is linked to the apprenticeship system, we now demand that there be apprentices linked to the specific contracts. It is an important objective that the new law should be manageable for small contracting entities with limited administrative resources. Greater flexibility and less administrative burdens for both contracting entities and suppliers will benefit everyone, and in particular small and medium-sized enterprises. Electronic self-declaration allowing suppliers not to submit mass documentation unless they are given the assignment, and the breakdown of contracts is an example of this. There is little doubt that there is a tension between simplification and other important societal considerations. That is why it is essential to give a good priority, and I believe that we have. Norwegian business is in transition, and we need to create new jobs in both existing and new businesses. So public acquisitions amount to about 460 billion. kr annually. Increasing the level of innovation with a small share of this will have a major impact on growth in innovative suppliers. Innovation in public procurement is about putting contracting authorities first to focus on the needs of dialogue with the market, so that new solutions can be found for businesses. We have many good examples of how this can be done, but it's potential for more. The new law will give greater access to negotiation, and a brand new type of contract, innovation partnership, will be introduced to promote innovation. The government believes that the ideal providers of health and social services are characterised by a high level of competence and commitment to the services they provide. They have a long and proud history of providing – and not least they have often started up – important welfare services. The government is concerned with diversity and quality of welfare services. That is why we are particularly concerned about cooperation with NPISHs providing health and social services. We have continued and further developed the cooperation agreement with the non-profit sector on the delivery of health and social services, and with the KS as a party to the agreement. We have developed an action plan to improve the framework conditions for ideals. The government has also set down a sample assessing state compensation to non-profit institutions serving their public service pension costs. On the part of the government, we are concerned that, through simpler and more flexible rules, we provide good conditions for the ideals. The new regulation on public procurement will contain a separate chapter on health and social services. In this context, we will clarify the possibilities for long-term or ongoing contracts and explain that the implementation of competition may focus on conditions of service that are particularly important to users, such as quality, user involvement and continuity. These elements are traditionally very good for ideal businesses. In addition, the government wishes to make full use of the space for action provided by EEA legislation to facilitate the continued provision of health and social services by non-profit institutions serving households. The Economic and Social Committee has asked the Government to obtain an external legal investigation into the scope of action in order to ensure non-profit actors in the health and social field, including various forms of cooperation. In this context, the Government will consider the scope of action to reserve competitions for contracts for health and social services for non-profits and the possibility of various forms of cooperation with non-profits, as extended self-government, within the framework of EEA law. At the same time, the government will take advantage of the opportunities available to us to influence the development of justice in the EU in this field. Last but not least, the government has been committed to strengthening the enforcement of illegal direct acquisitions because experience shows that it does not function properly. The Government has therefore proposed to reintroduce the complaint subject of public acquisitions its competence to impose an infringement fee in the event of illegal direct acquisitions. The Economic and Social Committee has proposed some adjustments to the legislative text. These are mainly clarifications that are consistent with government policy and that were intended to be regulated in a similar manner in the regulations. I now look forward to putting in place a new and modernised acquisition law. It means a lot to the public, but it also means a lot to the private business.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e2646	I noticed that the representative Pollestad skipped proposal No. 4, which is perhaps the most important of the suggestions we make. But I'll come back to that. A central principle of Norwegian tax legislation is that taxation and tax access should take place where value creation takes place. The same principle underlies all international work against aggressive tax planning and international tax evasion. SV believes the same principle should also apply to Norwegian state-owned companies. That is the basis for proposal No. 4th@item: inlistbox Those who participate in or facilitate aggressive tax planning make tax receipts from others. Such behavior has great negative effects and is unethical. It should therefore be a minimum objective that state-owned companies integrate this whole idea into their business and investment strategies. Most of the corruption cases we have had recently related to Norwegian state-owned companies have involved transactions via closed jurisdictions. Luxleaks and Panama Papers show exactly the same thing. Closed jurisdictions, known as tax havens, are used to finance, among other things. illicit activities such as drug trafficking, arms trafficking, trafficking in human beings, circumvention of UN-approved economic sanctions, human rights. It can't be in Norway's interest to have a business there. SV has never said that those who have business associated with closed jurisdictions are automatically criminals, but we say that we can't actually know. There's a big difference. If we are to be sure that Norwegian state-owned companies act according to Norwegian values and standards, the taxation must be done where the value creation takes place, i.e. one does not move it to other places – and at least not to closed jurisdictions. This is why SV's most important proposal in this case is No. 4, which – unlikelyly – we stand alone about, ‘that all state-owned companies move their subsidiaries away from closed jurisdictions’. The other two proposals are in common with the Christian People's Party. They are about important steps towards more systematic alerting – as we know to be very important – reporting and follow-up by Norwegian authorities. These two proposals are soft, maybe we can call them, but at the same time very important for us to get information in the light, and that we know that there is a dynamic between owners and companies. We also support the Workers' Party's proposal for a greater review of how state-owned companies work on these things, how governments follow it, and a strategy for more systematic work on this. We are in favour of that proposal too, but since the proposal was put into place so late in the process, we or some others in the committee did not have the opportunity to enter into it during the committee procedure. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e2673	Let me first say that transparency about cash flows is an important and demanding topic. We have therefore formulated clear expectations in the ownership notice about this, to which a total increase has joined. Money flows are a broad concept, covering both ordinary business activities, transactions, taxes and the ultimate possibility of corruption and tax evasion. Therefore, the means of achieving transparency of financial flows must be both to contribute to effective regulation nationally, internationally and to the transparency of reporting by companies themselves. I feel that we agree on the framework of state ownership, including how the state should exercise its ownership. The broad political consensus over several major periods is a strength of Norwegian state ownership. We follow up companies through ongoing dialogue and reporting. Deceptive governments have over time developed a form of work for the follow-up of government expectations to the work of governments in the field of social responsibility. These expectations are evident from the ownership message, which is broadly anchored in the Stortinget. Let me, among other things. mention: Of course, all directors are expected to have been thoroughly involved in the ownership message. The state has a regular ownership dialogue throughout the year, meeting where social responsibility is on the agenda, if necessary. We also expect companies' directors to report on significant issues in the social responsibility area in the ownership dialogue and in their annual reports. We have a follow-up or explain-principle. It implies that the Boards are expected to explain why, if the companies deviate from the expectations of the State, for which there may in some cases be grounds. Social responsibility is the theme of seminars and training that we organise for directors and new board members. I have a meeting next week with all the directors on anti-corruption and other business ethics issues, and I held a similar meeting in 2014. The Board of Directors is responsible for the management of the company, for taking current business decisions and for following up the expectations expressed by the State through the ownership notification. The State which holds its interests through the general meeting and by ensuring that the companies have a well-composed and competent board of directors. We're clear on our expectations, and we're following these up, but as owner, we don't control the companies. That's what other authorities do, e.g. Financial supervision and tax authorities. We regularly work to further develop government expectations in the area of social responsibility and how these are followed up in the ownership dialogue. In the last ownership message, we are among others. clear on the responsibilities of the Management Board in the field and on the principle of materiality. More specific expectations of anti-corruption work were also established. We also have involvement with PwC this spring. to advise on our follow-up to the ownership message and expectations of anti-corruption work. These include: in order to increase the systematicity of the follow-up of the Department. This important theme is also highly relevant to international cooperation and legislation. International cooperation and the development of good regulation are probably the main means of ensuring transparency of money flows and taxes. The government has referred to the work done in this area in the recent financial communication. Extensive international efforts are being made to prevent tax evasion and the Norwegian authorities have had an active driving role in the work for a number of years. Norway currently has a wide network of tax agreements and other administrative assistance agreements between tax authorities, all of which facilitate the exchange of information for tax purposes. In May 2016, the government promoted a legislative proposal that obliges large multinational groups to deliver a country-by-country report for tax purposes to the tax authorities. In July 2014, the OECD adopted a new standard for automatic exchange of financial account information for tax purposes and Norway has signed it. There are measures covering the whole of business and not just companies owned by the country. I believe that we have an active and thorough approach to our ownership follow-up in social responsibility, both through the communication of ownership and in the follow-up to it. At the same time, we're always concerned about improving. I believe that the most important work in this area is through international cooperation and the exchange of rules. Here Norway is an active driver, and work is a high priority.

For society as a whole, it is important that people who have previously been punished and settled separately can return to work. Many criminal acts are not being prosecuted or solved. A police record will only provide information on criminal offences from the period during which a person has been registered as resident in Norway. A police record will not be able to say anything about the reputation of people from their time outside Norway. Therefore, a police record without comments does not guarantee that he has never committed a criminal offence or that he will not commit a criminal offence in the future. On the other hand, it can be assumed that a number of persons convicted of a criminal offence can never imagine committing a criminal offence again. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e3071	I am pleased that the Stortinget can finally adopt the amendment to the law that clarifies the right to a sick place. In such cases, the patient shall be assigned a hospitalisation site and cannot be placed on a waiting list. The right shall also include stays in organised dwellings with assistance and supervision similar to those in a nursing home. The legislative proposal therefore also contains legislative changes to facilitate a better health care provision system. It will ensure a more predictable legal position for the individual and more transparency of the allocation process. On the basis of the proposal, each municipality shall draw up a regulation setting out the criteria for granting long-term residence in a hospital or similar housing in the municipality. The municipality must therefore be clear to its inhabitants as to what services they can expect in their own municipality. The government will introduce long-term national criteria following the implementation of the State funding experiment for care services, and we have gained experience with the national criteria there. It is important to provide the individual user and patient with predictability and certainty that he or she has been assessed in the light of the municipality’s criteria for granting long-term residence. A decision may contribute to such security while also providing legal certainty and the possibility to appeal on the basis of a clear decision. In the legislative proposal, we also propose that patients or users who are able to wait in a long-term place with reasonable assistance but who fulfil the criteria of the municipality should be entitled to such a decision. The municipality shall maintain waiting lists of patients and users waiting for long-term employment in the municipality. The model provides security for individual needs to be seen. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e3362	A new municipal patient and user registry will be of great importance for patients to experience an overall patient cycle, for us to be better able to prioritise health resources properly, and to provide us with a better knowledge base for quality improvement work. The Committee stresses that there is a strong political desire for a register that can provide equivalent knowledge of primary health services, as we do today about the specialist health service. It is therefore necessary to design the register in such a way that it will also provide relevant knowledge of the transfer of patients between these two levels. An important prerequisite for this is that the new municipal register is built over the same reading as the Norwegian patient register, which is the specialist health service register. The aim of the register is to provide those responsible at different levels with knowledge of the functioning of the services, whether they provide desired results, and whether patients, users and relatives experience services as whole and good. The register must be built up to provide information on the occurrence and cause of disease, disability and social problems, knowledge of effective prevention, treatment, rehabilitation and good ways to organise and finance services. The new municipal patient and user registry must take into account that the planning perspective is more long-term in the municipal health service, and that patient pathways are often long-term and involve several actors. The new municipal register must therefore focus more on function and process than on single diagnosis, individual services and individual treatment. User involvement and user preferences must also be weighted. The register must cover the activities of fixed doctors, medical practitioners, nurses and others engaged in diagnostics and treatment on a very large scale. The aim is for the register to cover the entire primary health field. We are currently lacking systematic knowledge of dental services for adults, health and school health services and health and care services for prisoners in prison. The new register shall be based on existing registers, such as: IPLOS. Data from IPLOS are not available for local learning and development work. The Committee is clear that the new municipal patient and user register is being changed so that relevant data are made available locally and become a basis for quality work. The Committee is committed to reducing the reporting burden and expects a critical review of the relevant governance data – which are realistic and maintain quality requirements. Electronic data capture from professional and patient records must be carried out as automatically as possible. The need for data will change as a result of the development of healthcare. Wide professional anchoring is important. Regulation and future changes will be consulted. There is disagreement in the Committee and among the hearings on the usefulness of a comprehensive register. There are also several who, for privacy reasons, are critical to collecting such comprehensive data on citizens, and there are differing views on whether the register should be based on consent or open to reservation rights. The minority is going to explain their vision later. The majority of the Committee emphasises the high value of a register which must cover, as far as possible, all relevant primary health care and demonstrates that the interests of privacy and unauthorised use of information will be safeguarded by legislation and technical and organisational solutions. The Ministry will also examine the possibility of reservation rights during regulatory work. The majority believe that the municipal health register must be based on consent, in the same way as the specialist health register. Good data quality is important in order to provide information on patient careers and health care needs before and after hospital discharge. For example, the majority to the Institute of Public Health, which considers that the non-compliance will be significant and not statistically evenly distributed among the population. The data base thus becomes neither complete nor valid and will present significant challenges for analysis and interpretation — and in the next round for priorities and quality in treatment provision. The majority of the Committee advises the Stortingen to adopt the proposed legislative amendments.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e3388	There is a great need and a strong political desire to establish a patient and user register that provides an equivalent knowledge of primary health services – as we do today about specialist health services. We also need a register that can provide knowledge of the relationship between the two levels and the transfer of patients between levels. The government is therefore proposing the establishment of a municipal patient and user register to improve information and knowledge about municipal health and care services. The municipal patient and user registers will be built over the same reading as the National Patient Register. It is essential to provide important information on the patient profile to patients in need of healthcare services, both before and after hospital discharge. Just as the Norwegian patient registry currently provides the basis for decisions in the specialist healthcare service, responsible primary health service providers need to have the necessary and relevant information on the services provided and their use in order to strengthen their own offerings. The main purpose of a municipal register of patients and users is to provide the basis for planning, management, financing and evaluation of municipal health and care services for central and municipal authorities. The information contained in the register shall also be used for statistics, health analysis, quality improvement, preparedness, prevention and research. Knowledge of municipal health and care services — as it works today — is fragmented and not suitable for planning and managing healthcare. It is therefore high time to put in place a better knowledge base on municipal services, if we are to achieve the ambitions to increase the quality of care services in municipalities. To this end, both central and municipal health authorities will need more knowledge of the services already provided. The government has a strong focus on creating patients’ and users’ health and care services. A new municipal patient and user register will help each user to obtain better services. The proposal for a municipal patient registry is a follow-up to the Report. St. 26 for 2014–2015 Primary Health Service of the Future – proximity and completeness and Report. St. 19 for 2014–2015 Public Health Communication – Mestring and opportunities. They provide the basis for re-use, organisation and compilation of existing data, from the services of a single register. The register will be established on the basis of existing information and provide updates on the occurrence and cause of disease, disability and social problems. All of this will be important in order to better prevent and expand service provision with the right capacity. When changing the Health Register Act — as the government now suggests — the privacy of patients and users will be protected. The changes shall also not result in an increased need for reporting. It is worth noting that the National Register of Patients for Specialist Health is also not consent-based or reserved. A majority of the hearing bodies considered that a municipal patient and user register cannot be based on consent either. Many also showed that it is particularly important that municipal patient and user registers have representative data. In the Public Health Institute consultation opinion, it was also shown that society spends considerable resources on health and care services, which also shows the need for a good and robust knowledge base for the best planning, governance, funding and evaluation of these services by central and municipal authorities. The government will provide the patient’s healthcare, and we are all committed to strengthening municipal health and care services. If we are to succeed, it is important that health authorities be given the opportunity to acquire a better knowledge base on services and to ensure that resources are used in a good way, providing good quality and health care to the individual patient and user.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e3477	A municipal patient and user register will provide us with knowledge we need to be able to create the patient’s and the user’s healthcare service. Good services with the right capacity require us to know the causes and incidence of illness, disability and social problems. We need to know what provides good services, and what are good ways to organize and finance them. How do the services work? Do we get what we want? What is the interaction between municipal and specialist health services? This is knowledge that both municipal and central health authorities need. The proposed register will provide that type of knowledge. It will also provide patients and users with insight into the activities and results of healthcare. This specific bill is to amend Section 11 of the Health Register Act so that we can establish a municipal patient and user register in a regulation. The aim is to provide a basis for the planning, governance, financing and evaluation of municipal health and care services for central and municipal authorities. This purpose shall govern the information for which the register may contain. The information contained in the register shall also be used for statistics, health analysis, quality improvement, preparedness, prevention and research. The main action is to facilitate the re-use, organisation and compilation of existing information from the municipal services in a comprehensive register. The aim is not to increase the reporting burden on the registry. In the first instance, the registry shall be based on the existing health reimbursement and reimbursement system, KUHR, and the national health registry for individual care and care services, IPLOS. I take seriously the fact that the registration and storage of health information about individuals can in themselves be stressful, and that some people may be concerned that the information may be diverted. The privacy of patients and users must, of course, be safeguarded. The health or social benefits of the register, as I have explained in the proposal, will clearly exceed the data protection disadvantages. The information shall be processed in accordance with information security, professional secrecy, access rights, logging, the access of data subjects to the log and other data protection requirements of the Health Registry Act. Use of data for planning, management, evaluation, statistics, research, etc. will take place mainly on health data not related to the birth number. However, registration of the birth number is necessary in order to ensure good register quality, to develop good data sets and to link the data in the course of the process between the municipal health service and the specialist health service. The birth number shall be stored encrypted and separately from the health data. Name will not be registered. This will provide a very good assurance that the information will not be disseminated to unauthorised persons. I refer you to the Norwegian Patient Register and the Heart and Cardiovascular Register, which have a similar solution. We have a very good experience with this solution. Getting the most complete data is essential. Although there will be a few who make reservations, there are likely to be large local differences and those who make reservations have special characteristics. This could lead to a biased non-response and affect the quality of the register. It must therefore be possible to obtain and record information without requiring the consent or granting of a general reservation right. Nor is it possible to make reservations about registration in the KUHR or similar registers, such as the Norwegian Patient Register. Should a universal reservation right have been introduced, this would have to be established as a new register of new reporting, thus introducing double and triple work in the service. Self-determination of own data is important for privacy. I would therefore like to consider in the work on the regulation whether it is possible to reserve itself against the registration of some information or against use for certain purposes. The purpose and content of the register will be further regulated by the regulation. Detailed rules on the disclosure of information from the register shall also be laid down. Draft regulation will be sent at the hearing. A regulation provides an opportunity to get input from users, organisations and institutions that have views on these issues. A new municipal register of patients and users will help individuals to obtain better services and to develop the healthcare and care of patients and users. I am absolutely convinced that this register will help the management of municipalities and local politicians to be clear on the need for quality of service.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e3560	All of them first: let me thank the Committee for a clean and good process when dealing with this proposition. There is a broad majority that stands united behind most of the content of the proposition. It is gratifying that the country’s National Assembly is finally taking a decision that has been debated for many years, but no one – uncertainly for what reason – has been able to realise in the past. The Proposition is a result of the Government Party's Declaration of Health Violence, which states that it will: ‘open for product samples and restricted alcohol sales directly from niche producers and, if necessary, work to amend EU legislation in the field’. It is a joyous day for producers who will now have the opportunity to generate income from a new secondary industry. This is because today we are amending the alcohol law so that it is opened for sale directly from the producer of products above 4,7 % by volume of alcohol, which are not covered by the EEA Agreement and which have not been added spirits or mixed with other alcoholic beverages. Let me stress that the new scheme is based on the fact that it does not affect the position of the wine monopoly. A majority of the hearing bodies have supported the proposal and believe that the scope of the scheme will be so small that it will not pose a threat to the consistency of the Norwegian alcohol policy, nor would it pose a threat to the monopoly system. This is primarily an opportunity for niche producers to offer their products for sale at their own sales premises. The construction shall be part of an offer of a holistic nature and a minimum of one third of the own-produced raw materials shall be used. Such a system will be able to complete the offer of farm tourism, for example. Visitors can buy local products without having to walk the road to the Vinmonopolet. The Norwegian Bondelag’s hearing response states: ‘The main parts of the sale are likely to be to foreign tourists’.» It is easy to draw parallels to vineyards in France, Spain or Germany — where Norwegian tourists have always been able to do just that. The laws we adopt today represent simplification, are customer-friendly and generate new income – and perhaps even new jobs. The majority of the Committee has chosen to support the government's wish for a volume limitation of 15 000 litres per producer per year. There is a reduction from 20,000 litres from the time the case was being consulted — a reduction that has been made after the many recordings in the consultation round. The member of the Committee from the Central Party has proposed a volume limit of 10 000 litres, while the member of the Committee from the Christian People's Party has been relieved of the reduction in volume, but considers that despite this the proposals cannot be defended as they exist. In my view, 15 000 litres will be sufficient to provide sufficient room for innovation of new product types and adequate opportunities for new growth, as well as to meet the demand of a sales outlet in relation to production, and where production and sales are part of the overall nature and supply. The majority of the Committee, all parties other than the Christian People's Party, sees that the proposed amendments to the alcohol law could serve as a secondary industry specifically related to farm tourism. I believe that this amendment to the law is not a liberalisation of the alcohol law, but an organisation for a better tourist offer and an opportunity for a successful secondary industry for small producers. There is no secret that the HANEN member companies – the trade union organisation for rural tourism, farm food and inland fishing – have been expected to deal with this proposition by the Committee. The same thing has Hardanger Side producer team, who has been fighting for this case for very many years, and now finally soon can offer pages designated as the champagne from Hardanger for sale from their own farm, which can create ground to develop both the fruit industry and travel life in the region. They will all be pleased with the decisions we are going to make in this matter tonight. The law will come into force from 1 July, and then it will only remain to apply for municipal funding for those who want to produce and sell products according to the legislation that will then give rise to.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e3708	I would like to refer to the Health Declaration, where it was stated that the government will: ‘open for product samples and restricted alcohol sales directly from niche producers and, if necessary, work to amend EU legislation in the field’. The government has followed this up with the proposed scheme, which will allow small producers to sell alcoholic beverages as a supplement to other industries. The main lines of the alcohol policy are fixed. This includes, inter alia: the preservation of the sales monopoly and the authorisation system for the sale and donation of alcoholic beverages. However, in the interests of food and product development, there is a need to look at the possibility of making adjustments that will increase little accessibility and challenge the monopoly system. The proposal to open up the sale of selected beverages above 4,7 % by volume of alcohol directly from the producer is such an adjustment. In Norway, we have a high level of protection justified on grounds of public health. This allows us to use, in part, extensive alcohol policy instruments to limit consumption and damage caused by consumption. This includes, inter alia: a strict ban on advertising, age limits, opening hours, authorisation schemes and the wine monopoly system. The justification for the wine monopoly in the EEA law is based on public health considerations and presupposes that the scheme is part of a restrictive, comprehensive and consistent alcohol policy. It has been very important in the formulation of the proposal to ensure a continuing high level of protection and preservation of the wine monopoly system. As part of the follow-up to the Government Platform, it was determined whether it would be possible to open up the sale of beverages with more than 4.7 % by volume of alcohol, without altering or weakening the current monopoly system. The conclusion is that the answer depends on the alcoholic beverages involved. For the trade in alcoholic beverages covered by the EEA Agreement, such as beer and spirits, it will be difficult to open up for sale without being contrary to EEA law. However, according to the investigators, opening up the sale of alcoholic beverages not covered by the scope of the EEA Agreement is unlikely to have an impact on EEA law. There is even greater reason to base this on restrictions on such a scheme, and that is what is done in the proposal. In this context, the proposal covers only products not covered by the EEA Agreement. In order to ensure that the restrictive Norwegian alcohol policy in general and the Vinmonopoly in particular are not challenged, the proposed system is limited. It is proposed that the products should not be made stronger by the addition of spirits and that the products may not be mixed with alcoholic products other than those proposed to be opened for sale. In addition, it is proposed to require one third of the inputs to be self-produced and it is proposed to impose a volume restriction on sales equivalent to 15000 litres per producer per year. The scale of the scheme is therefore assumed to be such that it cannot be said to have an impact on public health or on alcohol policy. We believe that the system will not challenge the consistency of the Norwegian alcohol policy, nor will it challenge the wine monopoly, either in fact or in law. In conclusion, I would like to point out that the Committee asks the Government to: ‘provide that the Vinmonopole facilitates, within the applicable legislation, the sale of products from local small-scale producers and that necessary adjustments and adjustments are made to purchase schemes, shops and online shops to ensure this.’ I will point out that the EEA Agreement obliges the Vinmonopolet to treat all producers in the EEA and EU territory in a non-discriminatory manner, and that therefore the Government cannot, or will not, instruct the Vinmonopolet to do so. However, I can assure the Committee that I am aware that the Vinmonopolet has already taken several measures to ensure that smaller producers and local products do not come out worse than others.

A SINTEF report from 2015 shows that there has been a reduction in the number of annual work in municipalities for mental health work for children and young people, from around 3 000 annual works in 2007 to approx. 2 500 in 2013, a decrease of approx. 13 pst. We need a systematic strengthening of services in the municipalities. In the field of mental health protection, we see that the waiting time for children and young people has increased in several health regions. It's worrying. Psychiatric health protection for children and young people must be provided with the necessary resources for both daily and daily services, and more intensive attention must be paid to outpatient teams following up children and young people where they are located. An overall committee asks the government to consider a requirement that municipalities define who has the overall responsibility for the health of young people so that they do not become caste balls in the aid system. Responsibility must be clarified and cooperation between service areas strengthened. Unfortunately, too many young people are telling us that they are being offered pills instead of someone to talk to. It is not acceptable to give young addictive drugs such as sleep medicine and antidepressants when professional assessments suggest that there is a need for psychological or other assistance. Therefore, a unanimous committee calls on the government to ensure that children and young people receive drug-free treatment when professional recommendations so require. It is therefore very gratifying that there is also agreement to introduce comprehensive and targeted measures to reduce the prescription of these drugs and the use of addictive drugs. Both the trade and user organisations gave full support to the proposal in the hearing. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e4301	Now it's about food. Thank you to the representatives of the Labour Party who have made this proposal. Malnutrition in the elderly is a known problem. Monitoring has shown that two out of three hospitals do not meet the requirements for good nutrition practices. The Health Directorate estimates that up to 60 pst. by the elderly in municipal health and care services are undernourished. In this proposal for a representative, this issue is addressed, with proposals for measures to improve this situation. In 2010, the Health Directorate provided national professional guidance on the prevention and treatment of malnutrition in healthcare services. In the guidelines, a recommendation and advice based on up-to-date knowledge is provided. These guidelines are intended as a tool, and the target group is staff and leaders in both the specialist and primary services. Following the guidelines, four simple measures will be used to win a lot: ‘1. To assess nutritional risk 2. Providing those at nutritional risk with a targeted nutritional care 3. To document nutritional status and measures in the patient's journal and epicrisis 4. Dissemination of documentation to the next level of care’» According to these guidelines, all hospital patients shall be assessed for nutritional risk at entry and every week thereafter. All residents of hospitals and institutions and persons receiving help in home care shall be assessed for nutritional risks at the time of entry or decision and then monthly thereafter. Persons at risk of nutrition shall have an individual nutrition plan with evidence of nutritional status, needs, intakes and measures. It's been six years since these guidelines came, and now it's been announced that they will be evaluated. The question is whether hospitals and hospitals have actually implemented these guidelines for good nutrition practices. There is little use of resources to develop new circulars and guidelines if the health service does not have a system to implement the professional guidelines. The Committee writes in its opinion that it is well documented that the national professional guidelines for the prevention and treatment of malnutrition are not properly followed up in the services. Today, there is a lack of access to specialist expertise in the field of clinical nutrition in the specialist services, and in the primary care services, this competence is often absent. There is a great need for more settings for clinical nutrition physiologists in both sectors, and there is a significant need to increase the education element in nutrition — especially clinical nutrition — in primary, continuing and continuing training for Lêr and other health professionals. The Committee points out that healthcare providers and municipalities now need to ensure that nutrition becomes an integral part of the treatment provision in healthcare services. I guess that's where it's failed. In the committee setting, the parties are mostly a few. Proper and good nutrition for the elderly at home and in the home is crucial to ensuring good care for the elderly and good public health. Nutrition shall be an integral part of the provision of care services. This has been the subject of our hearing, and the proposal for a representative has received a great deal of support. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e4334	Food is important. The well-being and community involved in sharing a meal is important – regardless of age. Daily meals are a central part of the municipal care service’s mission, both in nursing homes and in home care. We have long traditions associated with food, where we find identity, belonging and fellowship with other people. But it's not always as easy to get a good frame around meals, for example. for the resident elderly lonely, and perhaps not for those living in nursing homes. In particular, many residents of the home have problems with appetite and are often in a situation of life that makes it difficult to get enough food. I think that it is particularly among this group that we find many underreported cases of malnutrition and malnutrition in the elderly population. The Stoltenberg II government put public health work at the top of the political agenda. The diet was also emphasised, for both young and older people. In 2007, a national survey on food, meals and nutrition was carried out at the country’s health home under the direction of the Health Directorate. The report Food and meals in the hospital provided an overview of how meals and food are taken care of. The study gave a good picture of the situation at the time and showed, among other things: greater attention and awareness of the nutritional content of the food served are needed. In addition, it was argued that there is a need for more knowledge of the nutritional status of the individual user or recipient. The Health Directorate, as mentioned by the rapporteur, published in 2010 national professional guidelines for good nutrition, with clear recommendations and good tools to identify, prevent and treat malnutrition in hospitals and home care. Guides for good nutrition work were also developed for different types of care services. These relate to the production of food in the institutional kitchen, different forms of serving, monitoring of food and drink intake and how to assess and monitor the nutritional status. National health oversight had a national focus on the supervision of services for the elderly between 2009 and 2012, and a summary in 2013 showed that there had been a improvement in the nutrition sector for the elderly. Nevertheless, a survey carried out by the Consumer Council in June 2015 by 200 municipalities across the country showed that we need to go closer. Some municipalities spend four times as much money on food for the elderly as others. There is no link between the size of municipalities and the variation in the use of money. Based on several studies, the Health Directorate believes that 20-60 pst. are undernourished or at risk by the elderly care users. Therefore, it is important that all municipalities have a meal policy that ensures that the elderly are properly cared for. Each recipient of care services must be offered a tasty and nutritious food offering. This is supported by several large organisations: the National Public Health Association, the Medical Association, the Pensioners' Association and the Norwegian Confederation of Associations, to name a few. The Workers' Party is committed to promoting a policy for the care of the elderly that can reduce the difference in quality in the provision of services to municipalities. We believe that good nutrition needs to be taken into account in the care of the elderly and propose the development of a national diet and nutrition strategy for nursing homes and home care. Both research and user studies need to evaluate the experience of different production methods and different preparation and serving solutions. We also need up-to-date knowledge of the nutritional status of each user of care services, so that we can take action in line with the adopted national guidelines on malnutrition in the elderly. We think it is important in this respect to have sufficient competence in the field of nutrition, for example. from nutrition physiologists and ensuring that these are accessible to municipalities. The working party is aware that the government will develop an action plan for diet and nutrition between 2017 and 2022. We would like to see a comprehensive national diet strategy for the elderly involved or seen in connection with this work.

Being able to enjoy good food and taste requires knowledge of food, but also knowledge of dental health, disease, medications and mental health, which is also linked to food intake and appetite. Older people, whether they live in a nursing home or at home, often lose their spouse, friends and social relations, which means that their appetite too disappears, they don't get enough in them, and they get sick. Therefore, the Christian People's Party is concerned about and believes it is fundamentally important that every nursing home should have its own kitchen. Smelling, smelling, long before the food comes to the table, can do something about the food experience and appetite. We often talk about personalised medicine in the health field. But personalised food means a lot to make the food taste, especially for those who are sick, for those who have no appetite. Nor is there any doubt that knowledge of the importance of nutrition should have a clear place in all health professionals. Some people today have it in their professional education, but I'm afraid we should get it more in place in all professional groups. Had we been trained, there would not have been a problem with malnutrition in Norwegian nursing homes or home care. But precisely because we have more to learn, this should have a big focus. We will not succeed in habilitation and rehabilitation if we do not succeed in nutrition. Nor will we succeed completely with medication alone if we do not adapt medication to nutrition. A wise woman has said in this room: Everything is related to everything. That's also the case with food and nutrition, sickness and the elderly. If we don't get in just enough food, it goes beyond our physical and mental health. Nutrition is a central part of creating a complete and good offer. In the rest of the world, both hospitals and hospitals may use this even more than we do. They dedicate food and prohibit almost food from various diseases, because there are some things that are not related. We have a lot to learn.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-09.ana.ud2114e4425	Food and meals for the elderly are an important part of the municipal care service mission. Food is of great importance to the experience of quality of life and good health, and to the ability to be active in everyday life. Sub-nutrition increases the risk of complications, weakens resistance, exacerbates physical and cognitive function, delays convalescence and increases mortality. Several measures have been put in place to support municipalities in ensuring good nutrition and nutrition for elderly people receiving hospital and home care services. Surveys and supervisory experience show that improvements have been made in these areas in recent years. According to the Directorate-General for Nutritional Work in Health and Welfare, good nutrition work is primarily about the interest and competence of diet, knowledge of food that is cheap and healthy, and priorities of what you want to focus on. This government is putting a lot of emphasis on competence and knowledge. We have presented a separate plan for recruitment, competence and professional development in the municipal health and care services – Competencelift 2020. The aim of the plan is to contribute to professional strength and the adequate competence of municipal health and care services in nutrition as well. In 2016, we strengthened the municipal competence and innovation grant to give municipalities the opportunity to carry out skills, innovation and development work in the field of nutrition. The grant shall be managed by the county council. The county is an important driving force for good nutrition work in the municipalities and includes, among other things: initiatives for courses and seminars on good nutrition practices. The national effort to strengthen nutrition skills in the care service ended in 2015. The initiative has contributed to the development of different competences in municipalities, such as e-learning courses, training packages and guides. This is now spreading to other municipalities. Increased knowledge of the nutritional situation of persons receiving health and care services is important. The Health Directorate is therefore developing quality indicators for nutrition in the care service. The first indicators will be published in 2017 and will include, inter alia: view the number of persons assessed for nutritional status on arrival at hospital or by decision on home care, the proportion assessed for nutritional risk and the proportion of those at nutritional risk who have received measures; The government has started work on the development of a health home quality safety standard. The safety standard shall operationalise legal requirements and other quality standards in order to facilitate their implementation by Norwegian hospitals. The quality regulation, such as: is about nutrition, will have a natural place in this work. The system and standard shall be tested in a selection of municipalities until spring 2018. The evaluation will form the basis for further development and implementation of the safety standard. From this year on, nutrition has become a new input into the national patient safety programme, in safe hands. Work is under way on developing a concrete action package on nutrition. Following the piloting of the action package, learning networks are organised to disseminate and implement the action package. The Health Directorate has decided to revise the National Professional Guideline to prevent and treat malnutrition. In the guideline, it is clearly required that all those attending health and care services have their nutritional status and receive an individualised offer. Such a measure is important for the prevention and treatment of diet-related diseases and malnourishment and malnutrition. The Ministry of Agriculture and Food also cooperates with the Ministry of Agriculture and Food, which has initiated different courses, conferences or competitions for staff in the care services for the past four years. In dealing with public health messages, Mestring and opportunities, Stortinget adopted a new action plan with concrete measures to promote healthy diets across the population. Children, young people and older people are the priority target groups. The Ministry of Health and Welfare coordinates this work between the different departments and aims at the completion of the action plan in 2017.

The proposal concerns the first and third paragraphs of Section 12 of the Recommendation, where the Committee has made proposals for a correction of the text compared to the wording of the referral. The proposal reads as follows: ‘In the law on public procurement, the first paragraph of paragraph 12 shall be replaced by the following: If the dispute settlement body finds that the contracting entity or any person acting intentionally or seriously negligently on its behalf has made an illegal direct acquisition, the body shall impose an infringement fee on the contracting entity. [[purchasing law]] If the body finds that the contracting entity or any person acting negligently on its behalf has made an illegal direct acquisition, it may impose an infringement fee on the contracting entity. Paragraph 12 shall be replaced by the following: in determining whether an infringement fee shall be imposed in accordance with the second sentence of the first paragraph of Section 12 and in determining the amount of the fee, particular attention shall be paid to the gravity of the infringement, the amount of the acquisition, whether the payer has made repeated illegal direct acquisitions and the preventative effect. The fee may not be higher than 15 per cent of the value of the acquisition.»
